\langle a,M,\bot,M\rangle}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{E-FailLoop}

Administrative rules

\frac{M\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{M}}M^{\prime}}{\langle a,E[M],\sigma,\kappa\rangle\longrightarrow\langle a,E[M^{\prime}],\sigma,\kappa\rangle}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{E-LiftM}\par\frac{\mathcal{C}\equiv\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\hskip 8.50012pt\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\equiv\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{E-Equiv}\par\frac{\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\parallel\mathcal{D}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{E-Par}\par\frac{\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}}{(\nu n)\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow(\nu n)\mathcal{D}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{E-Nu}

Configuration equivalence

\mathcal{C}\equiv\mathcal{D}

\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D}\equiv\mathcal{D}\parallel\mathcal{C}\par\mathcal{C}\parallel(\mathcal{D}\parallel\mathcal{E})\equiv(\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D})\parallel\mathcal{E}\par(\nu n_{1})(\nu n_{2})\mathcal{C}\equiv(\nu n_{2})(\nu n_{1})\mathcal{C}\par\mathcal{C}\parallel(\nu n)\mathcal{D}\equiv(\nu n)(\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D})\hskip 8.50012pt\text{if }n\not\in{\textsf{fn}}(\mathcal{C})\par(\nu s)(\lightning s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}_{1}]\parallel\cdots\parallel\lightning s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}_{n}])\parallel\mathcal{C}\equiv\mathcal{C}\par\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathbf{0}\equiv\mathcal{C}

Figure 15
:
Operational semantics (3)

Term reduction
\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{M}}
is standard
\beta
-reduction, save for

E-TryRaise
which evaluates the failure continuation in the case of an
exception.
We consider four
subcategories of configuration reduction rules: actor and adaptation rules; session
communication rules; exception handling rules; and administrative rules.

Actor / adaptation rules.

Given a
fully-evaluated actor,
E-Loop
runs the term specified by the actor’s
behaviour.
Rule
E-New
allows actor
a
to spawn a new actor of class
u

by creating a fresh runtime actor name
b
and a new actor process of the form

\langle b,M,\bot,M\rangle
where
M
is the behaviour specified by
u
,
returning the process ID
b
.
Rules
E-Replace
and
E-ReplaceSelf
handle replacement by
changing the behaviour of an actor, returning the unit value to the caller.
Rule
E-Discover
returns the process ID of an actor
b
if it has the
desired static session type
S
.
Rule
E-Self
returns the PID of the local actor.

Session communication rules.

An actor begins a session by connecting to another actor while disconnected;
such a case is handled by rule
E-ConnInit
. Suppose we have a
disconnected actor
a
evaluating a connection statement

{\textbf{{connect}}}\;\ell_{j}(V)\;{\textbf{{to}}}\;{b}\;{\textbf{{as}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
, evaluating in parallel with a disconnected
actor
b
evaluating an accept statement
{\textbf{{accept}}}\;{\textbf{{from}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\;\{\ell_{i}(x_{i})\mapsto M_{i}\}_{i\in I}
. Rule
E-ConnInit
returns the unit value to actor
a
;
creates a fresh session name restriction
s
, sets the connection
state of
a
to
s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\rangle
and of
b
to

s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\rangle
;
accepting actor
b
then evaluates continuation
M_{j}
with
V

substituted for
x_{j}
.
Since exception handlers only scope over a single communication action, the
top-level frames
F,F^{\prime}
in each actor are discarded if the
communication succeeds.
Rule
E-Conn
handles the case where the connecting actor is already part
of a session and behaves similarly to
E-ConnInit
, without creating a
new session name restriction.
A connection can fail if an actor attempts to connect to another actor which is
terminated or is
already involved in a session; in these cases,
E-ConnFail
raises an
exception in the connecting actor.

Rule
E-Disconn
handles the case where an actor
b
leaves a session,
synchronising with an actor
a
. In this case, the unit value is returned to
both callers, and the connection state of
b
is set to
\bot
.
Rule
E-Comm
handles session communication when two participants are
already connected to the same session, and is similar to
E-Conn
.
Rule
E-Complete
garbage collects a session after it has completed and
sets the initiator’s connection state to
\bot
.

Exception handling rules.

Exception handling rules allow safe session communication in
the presence of exceptions. Rule
E-CommRaise
states that if an actor is
attempting to communicate with a role no longer present due to an
exception, then an exception should be raised. We write
{\textsf{subj}}(E[M])={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
if

M\in\{{\textbf{{send}}}\;\ell(V)\;{\textbf{{to}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}},{\textbf{{receive}}}\;{\textbf{{from}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\;\{\ell_{i}(x_{i})\mapsto N_{i}\}_{i},{\textbf{{wait}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}},{\textbf{{disconnect}}}\;{\textbf{{from}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\}
.
Rule
E-FailS
states that if a connected actor encounters an unhandled
exception, then a zapper thread will be generated for the current role, the
actor will become disconnected, and the current evaluation context will be
discarded. Rule
E-FailLoop
restarts an actor encountering an
unhandled exception.

Administrative rules.

The remaining rules are administrative:
E-LiftM
allows term reduction
inside an actor;
E-Equiv
allows reduction modulo structural congruence;

E-Par
allows reduction under parallel composition; and
E-Nu

allows reduction under name restrictions.

Configuration equivalence.

Reduction includes configuration equivalence
\equiv
, defined as the smallest
congruence relation satisfying the axioms in
Figure
15
.
The equivalence rules extend the usual
\pi
-calculus structural congruence
rules with a ‘garbage collection’ equivalence, which allows us to
discard a session where all participants have exited due to an error.

5.4
Metatheory

We now turn our attention to showing that session typing allows runtime
adaptation and discovery while precluding communication mismatches and
deadlocks.

5.4.1
Runtime typing

To reason about the metatheory, we introduce typing rules for
configurations (
Fig.​ 16
): the judgement

\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\mathcal{C}
states that configuration
\mathcal{C}
is
well-typed under term typing environment
\Gamma
and runtime typing environment

\Delta
.

Runtime Typing Rules

\vphantom{\Delta;\mathcal{C}}{\Gamma}\vdash{V}{:}{A}

\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\mathcal{C}

\frac{\Gamma,a:{\textsf{Pid}}(S);\Delta,a:S\vdash\mathcal{C}}{\Gamma;\Delta\vdash(\nu a)\mathcal{C}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{T-Pid}\par\frac{\Delta^{\prime}=\{s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}_{i}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}_{i}\rangle{:}S_{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}_{i}}\}_{i\in I}\hskip 8.50012pt\varphi(\Delta^{\prime})\hskip 8.50012pts\not\in\Delta\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma;\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}\vdash\mathcal{C}\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\varphi\text{ is a safety property}}{\Gamma;\Delta\vdash(\nu s)\mathcal{C}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{T-Session}\par\frac{\Gamma;\Delta_{1}\vdash\mathcal{C}\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma;\Delta_{2}\vdash\mathcal{D}}{\Gamma;\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\vdash\mathcal{C}\parallel\mathcal{D}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{T-Par}\par\frac{}{\Gamma;s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\rangle{:}S\vdash{\rm ZAp}{s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{T-Zap}\par\frac{}{\Gamma;\cdot\vdash\mathbf{0}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{T-Zero}\par\frac{T=S\vee T={\textsf{end}}\hskip 8.50012pta:{\textsf{Pid}}(S)\in\Gamma\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\{S\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{T}\triangleright{M}{:}{A}\triangleleft{{\textsf{end}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\{S\}\>\>{\Gamma}\vdash{\kappa}}{\Gamma;a:S\vdash\langle a,M,\bot,\kappa\rangle}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{T-DisconnectedActor}\par\frac{a:{\textsf{Pid}}(T)\in\Gamma\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\{T\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S}\triangleright{M}{:}{A}\triangleleft{{\textsf{end}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\{T\}\>\>{\Gamma}\vdash{\kappa}}{\Gamma;a:T,s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\rangle{:}S\vdash\langle a,M,s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\rangle,\kappa\rangle}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{T-ConnectedActor}

Figure 16
:
Runtime typing rules

Rule
T-Pid
types actor name restriction
(\nu a)\mathcal{C}

by adding a PID into the term
environment, and extending the runtime typing environment
a:S
; the linearity
of the runtime typing environment therefore means that the system must contain precisely
one actor with name
a
.

Session name restrictions
(\nu s)\mathcal{C}
are typed by

T-Session
.
We follow the formulation of
Scalas and Yoshida 2019
which types multiparty
sessions using a parametric safety property
\varphi
; we discuss safety
properties in more depth in Section
5.4.2
.
Let
\Delta^{\prime}
be a
runtime typing environment containing only mappings of the form

s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}_{i}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}_{i}\rangle{:}S_{i}
. Assuming
\Delta
does not contain any mappings
involving session
s
and
\Delta^{\prime}
satisfies
\varphi
, the rule states that
\mathcal{C}
is typable under typing environment
\Gamma
and runtime typing environment
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}
.
It is sometimes convenient to annotate session
\nu
-binders with their
environment, e.g.,
(\nu s:\Delta^{\prime})\mathcal{C}
.

Rule
T-Par
types each subconfiguration of a parallel composition by
splitting the linear runtime environment. Rule
T-Zap
types a zapper
thread
{\rm ZAp}{s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]}
, assuming the runtime environment
contains an entry
s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\rangle{:}S
for any session type

S
.

Finally, rules
T-DisconnectedActor
and
T-ConnectedActor
type
disconnected and connected actor configurations respectively.
Given an actor with name
a
and static session type
T
, both rules require that
the typing environment contains
a:{\textsf{Pid}}(T)
and
runtime environment contains
a:T
.
Both rules require that the current session type is fully consumed by the
currently-evaluating term and that the actor’s behaviour should be typable under

T
.
Rule
T-DisconnectedActor
requires that the currently-evaluating term
must be typable under either
T
or
end
, whereas to type a
connection state of
s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\rangle
and current session type

S
,
T-ConnectedActor
requires an entry

s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\rangle{:}S
in the runtime environment.

5.4.2
Preservation

We now prove that reduction preserves typability and thus that actors only
perform communication actions specified in their session types. Due to our use
of explicit connection actions, classical MPST approaches are too limited for
our purposes. Our approach, following that of
Scalas and Yoshida 2019
, is to
introduce a labelled transition system (LTS) on local types, and specify a
generic safety property based around local type reduction. The property can then
refined; in our case, we will later specialise the property in order to prove
progress.

Labels

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\text{Labels}&\gamma&::=&s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}{\dagger}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{::}\ell(A)\,\mid\,s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{::}\ell\,\mid\,s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}{\ddagger}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\\
\text{Synchronisation labels}&\rho&::=&s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{::}\ell\,\mid\,s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{::}\ell\,\mid\,s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}{\#}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\end{array}

Reduction on runtime typing environments

:
Labelled transition system for runtime typing environments

Reduction on runtime typing environments.

Figure
17
shows the LTS on runtime typing environments.
There are two judgements:
\Delta\xrightarrow{\gamma}\Delta^{\prime}
, which handles
reduction of individual local types, and a
synchronisation
judgement

\Delta\xRightarrow{\rho}\Delta^{\prime}
.

Rule
ET-Conn
handles the reduction of role
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
,
where the choice session type contains a connection action

{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}{!!}{\ell_{j}}({A_{j}})\,{.}\,S^{\prime}_{j}
.
If
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}
has a statically-defined session type
\Sigma_{k\in K}({{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}{??}{\ell_{k}}({B_{k}})\,{.}\,T_{k})

which can accept
\ell_{j}
from from
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
, and the
payload types match, reduction
advances
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
’s session type,
adds
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
’s connected role set,
and introduces an entry for
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}
into the
environment. The reduction emits a label

s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{::}\ell_{j}
.

Given a role
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
connected to
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}
with a session choice containing a
send or receive action
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{\dagger}{\ell_{j}(A)}\,{.}\,S^{\prime}_{j}
,
rule
ET-Act
will
emit a label
s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}{\dagger}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{::}\ell_{j}(A_{j})
and advance the session
type of
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
.

Rule
ET-Wait
handles the reduction of
{\#}{\uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\,{.}\,S
actions,
s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\rangle{:}{\#}{\uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\,{.}\,S
, where
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
waits for
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}
to
disconnect: the reduction emits label
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{{\#}{\uparrow}}

and removes
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
’s connected roles. Similarly,
rule
ET-Disconn
handles disconnection, by emitting label

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{{\#}{\downarrow}}
and removing the entry from the
environment.

ET-Rec
handles recursive types, and the
ET-Cong
rules
handle reduction of sub-environments.

Rule
ET-ConnSync
states that connection is a synchronisation action,
and rules
ET-Comm
and
ET-Disconn
handle synchronisation
between dual actions in sub-environments, emitting synchronisation labels

s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{::}\ell
and
s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}{\#}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}

respectively. We omit the congruence rules for synchronisation actions.
We say that a runtime environment
reduces
, written
\Delta\Longrightarrow
, if
there exists some
\Delta^{\prime}
such that
\Delta\Longrightarrow\Delta^{\prime}
.

Safety.

A
safety property
describes a set of invariants on typing environments
which allow us to prove preservation. Since the type system is parametric
in the given safety property, we can tweak the property to permit or rule out
different typing environments satisfying particular behavioural properties;
however, we need only prove type preservation once, using the weakest safety
property. Our safety property is different to the safety property described
by
Scalas and Yoshida 2019
in order to account for explicit connection actions.

Definition 6

(Safety Property)
.

\varphi
is a
safety property
of runtime typing contexts
\Delta
if:

1.

\varphi(\Delta,s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle{:}\Sigma_{i\in I}(\alpha_{i}\,{.}\,S_{i}),s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{s}}}}\rangle{:}\Sigma_{j\in J}({{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}{?}{\ell_{j}}({B_{j}})\,{.}\,T_{j}))
implies that if

{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}{{!}}{\ell_{k}}({A_{k}})\in\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in I}
,
then
k\in J
,
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\in\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\in\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{s}}}}
, and
A_{k}=B_{k}
.

2.

\varphi(\Delta,s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle{:}\Sigma_{i\in I}(\alpha_{i}\,{.}\,S_{i}))
implies that
if
\alpha_{i}={{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}{!!}{\ell_{j}}({A_{j}})\in\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in I}
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\not\in\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}
,

s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{s}}}}\rangle\not\in{\textsf{dom}}(\Delta)
, and

{\textsf{ty}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}})=\Sigma_{k\in K}({{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}{??}{\ell_{k}}({B_{k}})\,{.}\,T_{k})

with
j\in K
and
A_{j}=B_{j}
.

3.

\varphi(\Delta,s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\rangle{:}\mu X.S)
implies

\varphi(\Delta,s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\rangle{:}S\{\mu X.S/X\})

4.

\varphi(\Delta)
and
\Delta\Longrightarrow\Delta^{\prime}
implies
\varphi(\Delta^{\prime})

A runtime typing environment is
safe
, written

{\textsf{safe}}(\Delta)
, if
\varphi(\Delta)
for a safety property
\varphi
.

Clause (1) ensures that communication actions between participants are
compatible: if
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
is sending a message with label
\ell
and payload type

A
to
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}
is receiving from
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
,
then the two roles must be connected,
and
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}
must be able to receive
\ell
with a matching payload.

Clause (2) states that if
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
is connecting to a role
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}
with label

\ell
, then

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}
should not already be involved in the session, and should be able to
accept from
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
on message label
\ell
with a compatible payload type.
The requirement that
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}
is not already involved in the session rules
out the
correlation
errors described in
Section
5.2.1
.
Clause (3) handles recursion, and clause (4) requires that
safety is preserved under environment reduction.

Concretising the safety property.

In order to deduce that a runtime typing environment
\Delta
is safe, we define

\varphi(\Delta)=\{\Delta^{\prime}\,\mid\,\Delta\Longrightarrow^{*}\Delta^{\prime}\}

and verify that
\varphi
is a safety property by ensuring that it satisfies all
clauses in
6
.

Properties on protocols and programs.

It is useful to distinguish active and inactive session types, depending on
whether their associated role is currently involved in a session, and identify
the initiator of a session.

Definition 7

(Active and Inactive Session Types)
.

A session type
S
is
inactive
, written
{\textsf{inactive}}(S)
, if
S={\textsf{end}}
or
S=\Sigma_{i\in I}({{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}{??}{\ell_{i}}({A_{i}})\,{.}\,{S_{i}})
. Otherwise,
S
is
active
, written
{\textsf{active}}(S)
.

Definition 8

(Initiator, unique initiator)
.

Given a protocol
P
, a role
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}:S_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\in P
is an
initiator
if

S_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}=\Sigma_{i\in I}(\alpha_{i}\,{.}\,S_{i})
, and each
\alpha_{i}
is a connection action

{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}{!!}{\ell_{i}}({A_{i}})
.
Role
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
is a
unique initiator
of
P
if

{\textsf{inactive}}(S_{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}})
for all

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\in P\setminus\{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}:S_{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}\}
.

A protocol is
well-formed
if it is safe and has a unique initiator.

Definition 9

(Well-formed protocol)
.

A protocol
P=\{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is
well-formed

if it has a unique initiator
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}
of type
S
and

{\textsf{safe}}(s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle\emptyset\rangle{:}S)
for any
s
.

By way of example, the online shopping protocol is well-formed:
\mathttbf{Customer}

is the protocol’s unique initiator, and it is straightforward to verify that

{\textsf{safe}}(s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{Customer}}}]\langle\emptyset\rangle{:}\,{\textsf{ty}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{Customer}}}))
.

Definition 10

(Well-formed program)
.

A program
(\overrightarrow{D},\overrightarrow{P},M)
is
well-formed
if:

1.

For each actor definition
D={\textbf{{actor}}}\;u\;{\textbf{{follows}}}\;S\;\{N\}\in\overrightarrow{D}
,
there exists some role
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\in\overrightarrow{P}
such that
{\textsf{ty}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}})=S
, and

\{S\}\>\>\cdot\,\mid\,{S}\triangleright{N}{:}{A}\triangleleft{{\textsf{end}}}

2.

Each protocol
P\in\overrightarrow{P}
is well-formed and has a distinct set of
roles

3.

The ‘boot clause’
M
is typable under the empty typing environment
and does not perform any communication actions:

\{{\textsf{end}}\}\>\>\cdot\,\mid\,{{\textsf{end}}}\triangleright{M}{:}{A}\triangleleft{{\textsf{end}}}

When discussing the metatheory, we only consider configurations defined with
respect to a well-formed program. Specifically, we henceforth assume that each
actor definition in the system follows a session type matched by a role in a
given protocol, assume each role belongs to a single protocol, and assume that
all protocols are well-formed.

Given a safe runtime environment, configuration reduction preserves typability;
details can be found in Appendix
B
.
We write
\mathcal{R}^{?}
for the reflexive closure of a relation
\mathcal{R}
.

Theorem 11

(Preservation (Configurations))
.

Suppose
\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\mathcal{C}
with
{\textsf{safe}}(\Delta)
and where

\mathcal{C}
is defined wrt. a well-formed program.
If
\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, then there exists some
\Delta^{\prime}
such that
\Delta\Longrightarrow^{?}\Delta^{\prime}
and
\Gamma;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.

Preservation shows that each actor conforms to its session type, and that
communication never introduces unsoundness due to mismatching payload types.

5.4.3
Progress

We now show a progress property, which shows that given

protocols
which satisfy a progress property, EnsembleS
configurations
do not get stuck due to deadlocks.

A
final
runtime typing environment contains a single, disconnected role
of type
end
, reflecting the intuition that all roles will eventually
disconnect from a protocol initiator.

Definition 12

(Final environment)
.

An environment
\Delta
is
final
, written
{\textsf{end}}(\Delta)
,

\Delta=\{s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\emptyset\rangle{:}{\textsf{end}}\}
for some
s
and

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
.

So far, we have considered
safe
protocols, which ensure the absence of
communication mismatches. We say that an environment
satisfies progress

if each active role can eventually perform an action, each potential send is
eventually matched by a receive, and non-reducing environments are final.
Let
{\textsf{roles}}(\rho)
denote the roles referenced in a
synchronisation label (i.e.,
{\textsf{roles}}(\rho)=\{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\}
for

\rho\in\{s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{::}\ell,s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{::}\ell,s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}{\#}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\}
).

Definition 13

(Progress (Runtime typing
environments))
.

A runtime typing environment
\Delta

satisfies progress
, written

{\textsf{prog}}(\Delta)
, if:

•

(Role progress) for each
s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}_{i}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}_{i}\rangle{:}S_{i}\in\Delta
s.t.
{\textsf{active}}(S_{i})
,
\Delta\Longrightarrow^{*}\Delta^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\rho}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\in{\textsf{roles}}(\rho)

•

(Eventual comm.)
if
\Delta\!\Longrightarrow^{*}\!\Delta^{\prime}\!\xrightarrow{s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}{!}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{::}\ell(A)}
,
then
\Delta^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\overrightarrow{\rho}}\Delta^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{?}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}{::}\ell(A)}
,
with
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\not\in{\textsf{roles}}(\overrightarrow{\rho})

•

(Correct termination)
\Delta\Longrightarrow^{*}\Delta^{\prime}\not\Longrightarrow
implies
{\textsf{end}}(\Delta)

The online shopping example satisfies progress, since all roles will always
eventually be able to fire an action once connected, and since all roles
disconnect, the non-reducing final environment will be of the form

s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{Customer}}}]\langle\emptyset\rangle{:}{\textsf{end}}
.

Definition 14

(Progress (Programs))
.

A well-formed program
(\overrightarrow{D},\overrightarrow{P},M)

satisfies progress

if each
P\in\overrightarrow{P}
has a unique initiator
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}
of type
S
and

{\textsf{prog}}(s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle\emptyset\rangle{:}S)
for any
s
.

It is useful to define a
configuration context

\mathcal{G}
as
the one-hole context
\mathcal{G}::=[~]\,\mid\,(\nu s)\mathcal{G}\,\mid\,\mathcal{G}\parallel\mathcal{C}
.
A
session
consists of a session name restriction and all connected actors
and zapper threads. Each well-typed configuration can be written as a sequence
of sessions, followed by all disconnected actors.

Definition 15

(Session)
.

A configuration is a
session

\mathcal{S}
if it can be written:

(\nu s)(\langle a_{1},M_{1},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}_{1}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}_{1}\rangle,\kappa_{1}\rangle\parallel\cdots\parallel\langle a_{m},M_{m},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}_{m}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}_{m}\rangle,\kappa_{m}\rangle\parallel{\rm ZAp}{s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}_{m+1}]}\parallel\cdots\parallel{\rm ZAp}{s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}_{n}]})

An actor is
terminated
if it has reduced to a value or has an unhandled
exception, and has the behaviour
stop
. An unmatched discover occurs when no
other actors match a given session type. An actor is
accepting
if it is
ready to accept a connection.

Definition 16

(Terminated actor, unmatched discover, accepting actor)
.

•

An actor
\langle a,M,\sigma,\kappa\rangle
is
terminated

if
M={\textbf{{return}}}\>V
or
M=E_{{\textsf{P}}}[{\textbf{{raise}}}]
, and
\kappa={{\textbf{{stop}}}}
.

•

An actor
\langle a,E[{\textbf{{discover}}}\;S],\sigma,\kappa\rangle
which is a subconfiguration
of
\mathcal{C}
has an
unmatched discover
if no other
non-terminated actor in

\mathcal{C}
has session type
S
.

•

An actor
\langle a,M,\sigma,\kappa\rangle

is
accepting
if
M=E[{\textbf{{accept}}}\;{\textbf{{from}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\;\{\ell_{j}(x_{j})\mapsto N_{j}\}_{j}]
for
some evaluation context
E
and role
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
.

Unhandled exceptions will propagate through a session, progressively cancelling
all roles. A
failed session
consists of only zapper threads.

Definition 17

(Failed session)
.

We say that a session
\mathcal{S}
is a
failed session
, written

{\textsf{failed}}(\mathcal{S})
, if

\mathcal{S}\equiv(\nu s)(\lightning s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}_{1}]\parallel\cdots\parallel\lightning s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}_{n}])
.

The key
session progress
lemma establishes the reducibility of each session which does not
contain an unmatched discover and is typable under a reducible runtime typing
environment.

Lemma 18

(Session Progress)
.

If
\cdot;\cdot\vdash\mathcal{C}
where
\mathcal{C}
does not contain an
unmatched discover,
\mathcal{C}\equiv\mathcal{G}[\mathcal{S}]
and
\mathcal{S}=(\nu s:\Delta)\mathcal{D}
with

{\textsf{prog}}(\Delta)
, and
\mathcal{S}
is not a failed session, then
\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow
.

There are several steps to proving Lemma
18
.
First,
we introduce
exception-aware
reduction on runtime typing environments,
which explicitly accounts for zapper threads at the type level, and show that
exception-aware environments threads retain safety and progress. Second, we
introduce
flattenings
, which show that runtime typing
environments containing only unary output choices can type configurations
blocked on communication actions, and that flattenings preserve environment
reducibility. Finally, we show that configurations typable under flat,
reducible typing environments can reduce. Full details can be found in
Appendix
B
.

We can now show our second main result: in the absence of unmatched discovers,
a configuration can either reduce, or it consists only of accepting and
terminating actors.

Theorem 19

(Progress)
.

Suppose
\cdot;\cdot\vdash\mathcal{C}
where
\mathcal{C}
is defined
wrt. a well-formed program which satisfies progress, and
{\textsf{prog}}(\Delta)
for
each
(\nu s:\Delta)\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
.
If
\mathcal{C}
does not contain an unmatched discover,
either

\exists\mathcal{D}
such that
\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}
, or

\mathcal{C}\equiv\mathbf{0}
, or

\mathcal{C}\equiv(\nu b_{1}\cdots\nu b_{n})(\langle b_{1},N_{1},\bot,\kappa_{1}\rangle\parallel\cdots\parallel\langle b_{n},N_{n},\bot,\kappa_{n}\rangle)

where each
b_{i}
is terminated or accepting.

The proof eliminates all failed sessions by the structural congruence
rules; shows that the presence of sessions implies reducibility
(Lem.
18
); and reasons about disconnected actors.

In addition to each
actor conforming to its session type (
Thm. 11
),
Theorem
19
guarantees that the system does not deadlock. It
follows that session types ensure safe communication.

Theorem
19
assumes the absence of unmatched discovers. This is not a
significant limitation in practice, however, as unmatched discovers can be mitigated with
timeouts, where a timeout would trigger an exception.

6
Related Work

Behavioural typing for actors.

Mostrous and Vasconcelos 2011
present the first theoretical account of session types in an actor
language; their work effectively overlays a channel-based session
typing discipline on mailboxes using Erlang’s
reference generation capabilities.

Neykova and Yoshida 2017a
use MPSTs to specify
communication in an actor system, implemented in Python.
Fowler
[
21
]
implements similar ideas in Erlang,
with extensions to allow subsessions
[
17
]
and failure handling.

Neykova and Yoshida 2017b
later improve the recovery
mechanism of Erlang by using MPSTs to calculate a minimal set of affected roles.
The above works check multiparty session typing dynamically. We are first to
both formalise and implement static multiparty session type checking for an
actor language.

Active objects (AOs)
[
15
]
are actor-like concurrent objects
where results of asynchronous method invocations are returned through futures.

Bagherzadeh and Rajan 2017
study
order types
for an AO calculus, which
characterise causality and statically rule out data races. In contrast to MPSTs,
order types work bottom-up through type inference.

Kamburjan et al. 2016
apply an MPST-like system to Core ABS
[
38
]
,
a core AO calculus;
they establish soundness via a translation to register automata rather than via an operational semantics.

de’Liguoro and Padovani 2018
introduce
mailbox types
, a type system for
first-class, unordered mailboxes. Their calculus generalises the actor
model, since each process can be associated with more than one mailbox.
Their type discipline allows multiple writers and a single reader for each
mailbox, and ensures conformance, deadlock-freedom, and for many programs,
junk-freedom. Our approach is based on MPSTs and is more
process-centric.

Non-classical multiparty session types.

MPSTs were introduced
by
Honda et al. 2008
.

Classical
MPST theories are grounded in binary duality: safety follows as
a consequence of
consistency
(pointwise binary duality of interactions
between participants), and deadlock-freedom follows from projectability from a
global type.

Unfortunately, classical MPSTs are restrictive: there are many protocols which
are intuitively safe but not consistent.
Scalas and Yoshida 2019
introduced the first

non-classical
multiparty session
calculus. Instead of ensuring safety using binary duality, they define
an LTS on local types and
safety property
suitable for proving type
preservation; since the type system is
parametric
in the safety property
(inspired by
Igarashi and Kobayashi 2004
in the
\pi
-calculus), the property can be
customised in order to guarantee different properties such as deadlock-freedom
or liveness.

Hu and Yoshida 2017
formalise MPSTs with explicit connection actions via an LTS on
types rather than providing a concrete language design or calculus; in our
setting, a calculus is vital in order to account for the impact of adaptation
constructs. A key contribution of our work is the use of non-classical MPSTs to
prove preservation and progress properties for a calculus incorporating MPSTs
with explicit connection actions.

Adaptation.

None of the above work considers adaptation. The literature on formal studies of
adaptation is mainly based on process calculi, without programming language
design or implementation. Bravetti
et al.

[
8
]
develop a
process calculus that allows parts of a process to be dynamically replaced with
new definitions. Their later work
[
9
]
uses temporal logic
rather than types to verify adaptive processes. Di Giusto and Pérez

[
18
]
define a session type system for the same process calculus,
and prove that adaptation does not disrupt active sessions.
Later,
Di Giusto and Pérez 2015b
use an event-based approach so that adaptation
can depend on the state of a session protocol.
Anderson and Rathke
[
2
]
develop an MPST-like calculus and study
dynamic software update providing guarantees of communication safety and
liveness. Differently from our work, they do not consider runtime discovery of
software components and do not provide an implementation.

Coppo
et al.

[
13
]
consider self-adaptation, in which a system
reconfigures itself rather than receiving external updates.
They define an MPST calculus with self-adaptation
and prove type safety. Castellani
et al.

[
11
]
extend

[
13
]
to also guarantee properties of secure information flow,
but neither of these works have been implemented.
Dalla Preda
et al.

[
51
]
develop the AIOCJ system based on
choreographic programming for runtime updates. Their work is implemented in the
Jolie language
[
44
]
, but they do not consider runtime discovery.

In this work we focus on correct communication in the absence of adversaries, and do not consider security. The literature on security and behavioural types is surveyed by Bartoletti
et al.

[
5
]
and could provide a basis for future work on security properties.

7
Conclusion and Future Work

Modern computing increasingly requires software components to
adapt
to
their environment, by
discovering
,
replacing
, and

communicating
with other components which may not be part of the
system’s original design. Unfortunately, up until now, existing programming
languages have lacked the ability to support adaptation both

safely
and
statically
.
We therefore asked:

Can a programming language support static (compile-time) verification of safe runtime
dynamic self-adaptation, i.e., discovery, replacement and communication?

We have answered this question in the affirmative by introducing EnsembleS, an
actor-based language supporting adaptation, which uses multiparty session types
to guarantee communication safety, using explicit connection
actions to invite discovered actors into a session.
We have demonstrated the safety of our system by proving type soundness
theorems which state that each actor follows its session type, and that
communication does not introduce deadlocks. Our formalism makes essential use of

non-classical
MPSTs.

Future work.

Currently, in both the theory and implementation, each actor only takes part in
a single session. Unlike dynamically-checked implementations of session typing
for actors
[
47
,
21
]
, this means that a message received by an actor in one
session cannot trigger an interaction in another (e.g., the Warehouse example
in
[
47
]
).
A key focus for future work will be to allow actors to partake in multiple sessions.

Currently, EnsembleS discovery and replacement requires type equality. We
envisage that we could relax this constraint to subtyping
[
54
]
or
perhaps bisimilarity on local types to increase expressiveness.

In order to avoid session correlation errors, we require that each role includes
at most a single top-level
accept
construct (c.f.
[
32
]
). It
would be interesting to investigate the more general setting, which would likely
require dependent types.

Appendix A
Global types and projection

In this section, we show how global types can be projected into the local types
used in the core formalism. Unlike in classic MPST works, it is not the case
that projection alone guarantees safety and deadlock-freedom in itself.
Rather, projection produces local types,
which can be validated separately as described in the main
body of the paper.

This section is mostly a straightforward adaptation of the
work of
Hu and Yoshida 2017
to our setting.

Meta-level definitions

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\textsf{isOutput}}(L)&\triangleq&L\in\{{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}{!}{\ell}({A}),{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}{!!}{\ell}({A})\}\\
{\textsf{isInput}}(L)&\triangleq&L\in\{{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}{?}{\ell}({A}),{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}{??}{\ell}({A})\}\\
{\textsf{subj}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}:\ell(A)),{\textsf{subj}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}}}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}}}:\ell(A)),{\textsf{subj}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}}}\#{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}}})&\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\\

Figure 18:
Global types and projection

Syntax of types.

Similar to the presentation of local types, the key difference between this
presentation of global types and standard MPST systems is that instead of having
branching of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{s}}}\{\ell_{i}:G_{i}\}_{i}
, where
a single role makes a choice, this presentation of global types provides
arbitrary summations of
global actions
. The standard sending action is
therefore
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}:\ell(A)\,{.}\,G
which involves role

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
sending message
\ell(A)
to
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}
.

A key technical innovation is
explicit connection actions

(
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}}}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}}}:\ell(A)
) which establish a connection between two roles

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}}}
by sending a label
\ell
. Conversely,

disconnection

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}}}\#{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}}}
is a directed
disconnection where
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
disconnects from
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}
waits for

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
’s disconnection.

Instead of assuming that all roles are connected at the start of the the
session, in order to communicate, each role must be connected explicitly. This
allows communication paths where one party isn’t involved (as in Hu and
Yoshida’s travel agent example).

Projection.

Global types can be
projected
at a role in order to obtain a local type.
In standard MPST systems, a global type is well-formed if it is closed,
contractive, and projectable at all roles. A well-formed global type is then
guaranteed to be deadlock-free.

Define
{\textsf{roles}}(G)
to be the set of roles contained within global type
G
.

The projection function

G\upharpoonright_{\Phi}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}}}
projects global type
G
at role
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}
, and is
parameterised by a set of recursion variables
\Phi
. The inclusion of the

\Phi
parameter allows the pruning of unguarded recursion variables.

Projection on unary choices, recursive types, type variables, and session
termination are standard. Disconnection ensures that the disconnecting role is
unused in the remainder of the protocol.

Now consider the case of projecting
n
-ary choices for
n>1
.
Suppose we have
\Sigma_{i\in I}(G_{i})\upharpoonright_{\Phi}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}}}
.
If
G_{i}\upharpoonright_{\Phi}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}}}={\textsf{end}}
for all
G_{i}
, then the result of the
projection is
end
(and similarly for a recursion variable
X
).

Otherwise, there will be some subset
J
of
I
such that each
L_{j}
for
j\in J
is a communication action, and each
L_{k}\in I\setminus J
projects to
either
end
or a recursion variable.
For each
j\in J
, there is a further condition: the choice must consist of all
send actions, or all receive actions. If the latter, then the receiver must be
the same in all branches. This will ensure syntactically well-formed local
types used in the paper.

Unfolding.

We define the 1-unfolding of a global type
G
,
{\textsf{unf}}(G)
, as

{\textsf{unf}}(\mu X.G)={\textsf{unf}}(G\{{\textsf{end}}/X\})
and defined recursively over
the other constructs.

Role enabling.

In order to ensure that projection produces syntactically well-formed local
types, we require global types to satisfy
role enabling
: intuitively,
this means that after a choice occurs, each role must be ‘activated’ by
receiving a message before performing any further communications. In turn, this
avoids mixed choice.

Let
R
range over sets of roles.

Role enabling

R\vdash G

\frac{{\textsf{subj}}(\pi)\subseteq R\quad R\cup{\textsf{obj}}(\pi)\vdash G}{R\vdash\pi\,{.}\,G}\par\frac{|I|>1\quad\exists{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\in R.\forall i\in I.{\textsf{subj}}(\pi_{i})=\{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\}\wedge\{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\}\cup{\textsf{obj}}(\pi_{i})\vdash G_{i}}{R\vdash\Sigma_{i\in I}(G_{i})}

Syntactic validity of projected local types.

Definition 20

(Initiator (global type))
.

We say that
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
is an
initiator
of
G
if
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\in{\textsf{roles}}(G)
and
G\upharpoonright{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}=\Sigma_{i\in I}(\pi_{i}\,{.}\,G_{i})
,
where each
\pi_{i}
is a connection action. We say that
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
is the

unique initiator
of
G
if it is the only initiator.

Given the above definitions, we can show that the set of projected local types
are syntactically valid.

Lemma 21
.

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
is the unique initiator of
G
.
If
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\vdash{\textsf{unf}}(G)
and
(G\upharpoonright{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}=L_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}})_{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\in{\textsf{roles}}(G)}
,
then each
L_{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}
is syntactically valid.

Proof sketch.

The result can be established by induction on the derivation of

G\upharpoonright_{\Phi}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}}}
.

Due to the partiality of the projection operation, disconnection actions
must only occur as unary choices, and role enabling coupled with the
conditions on the projection of non-unary choices preclude mixed choice.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs for Section
5

B.1
Preservation

Lemma 22

(Preservation (Terms))
.

If
\{T\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S}\triangleright{M}{:}{A}\triangleleft{S^{\prime}}
and
M\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{M}}N
, then
\{T\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S}\triangleright{N}{:}{A}\triangleleft{S^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
M\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{M}}N
.
∎

Lemma 23

(Preservation (Equivalence))
.

If
\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}\equiv\mathcal{D}
,
then
\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\mathcal{D}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
\mathcal{C}\equiv\mathcal{D}
.
∎

Lemma 24

(Substitution)
.

If
\{T\}\>\>\Gamma,x:A\,\mid\,{S}\triangleright{M}{:}{B}\triangleleft{S^{\prime}}
and
{\Gamma}\vdash{V}{:}{A}
, then

\{T\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S}\triangleright{M\{V/x\}}{:}{B}\triangleleft{S^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
\{T\}\>\>\Gamma,x:A\,\mid\,{S}\triangleright{M}{:}{B}\triangleleft{S^{\prime}}
.
∎

Lemma 25

(Subterm typability)
.

Suppose
\mathbf{D}
is a derivation of
\{T\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S}\triangleright{E[M]}{:}{A}\triangleleft{S^{\prime}}
.
Then there exists some subderivation
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
of
\mathbf{D}
concluding

\{T\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S}\triangleright{M}{:}{B}\triangleleft{S^{\prime\prime}}
for some type
B
and local type
S^{\prime\prime}
, where
the position of
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{D}
corresponds to that of the hole in

E
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
E
.
∎

Lemma 26

(Replacement)
.

If:

1.

\mathbf{D}
is a derivation of
\{U\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S}\triangleright{E[M]}{:}{A}\triangleleft{T}

2.

\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
is a subderivation of
\mathbf{D}
concluding

\{U\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S}\triangleright{M}{:}{B}\triangleleft{T^{\prime}}
, where the position of
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
in

\mathbf{D}
corresponds to that of the hole in
E_{{\textsf{P}}}

3.

\{U\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S}\triangleright{N}{:}{B}\triangleleft{T^{\prime}}

Then
\{U\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S}\triangleright{E[N]}{:}{A}\triangleleft{T}
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
E
.
∎

Due to the absence of exception handling frames which constrain the
pre-condition to match that of the failure continuation,
pure contexts admit a more liberal replacement lemma.

Lemma 27

(Replacement (Pure contexts))
.

If:

1.

\mathbf{D}
is a derivation of
\{U\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S}\triangleright{E_{{\textsf{P}}}[M]}{:}{A}\triangleleft{T}

\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
is a subderivation of
\mathbf{D}
concluding

\{U\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S}\triangleright{M}{:}{B}\triangleleft{T^{\prime}}
, where the position of
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
in

\mathbf{D}
corresponds to that of the hole in
E_{{\textsf{P}}}

3.

\{U\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S^{\prime}}\triangleright{N}{:}{B}\triangleleft{T^{\prime}}

Then
\{U\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S^{\prime}}\triangleright{E_{{\textsf{P}}}[N]}{:}{A}\triangleleft{T}
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
E_{{\textsf{P}}}
, noting that
S
is not constrained
by exception handling frames.
∎

See
11

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.

\footnotesize\frac{\frac{a:{\textsf{Pid}}(S)\in\Gamma\hskip 8.24454pt\{S\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S^{\prime}}\triangleright{E[{\textbf{{return}}}\>b]}{:}{A}\triangleleft{{\textsf{end}}}\hskip 8.24454pt\{S\}\>\>{\Gamma}\vdash{\kappa_{1}}}{\Gamma;a:S,s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle{:}S^{\prime}\vdash\langle a,E[{\textbf{{discover}}}\;T],s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle,\kappa_{1}\rangle}\hskip 8.50012pt\frac{b:{\textsf{Pid}}(T)\in\Gamma\hskip 8.24454pt\{T\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{T^{\prime}}\triangleright{M}{:}{B}\triangleleft{{\textsf{end}}}\hskip 8.24454pt\{T\}\>\>{\Gamma}\vdash{\kappa_{2}}}{\Gamma;b:T,t[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{s}}}}\rangle{:}T^{\prime}\vdash\langle b,M,t[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{s}}}}\rangle,\kappa_{2}\rangle}}{\Gamma;a:S,b:T,s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle{:}S^{\prime},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{s}}}}\rangle{:}T^{\prime}\vdash\langle a,E[{\textbf{{return}}}\>b],s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle,\kappa_{1}\rangle\parallel\langle b,M,t[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{s}}}}\rangle,\kappa_{2}\rangle}

as required.

Assumption:

, by case analysis on

, wee either
have:

\footnotesize\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}{!!}{\ell_{j}}({A_{j}})\,{.}\,S^{\prime}_{j}\in\{S_{i}\}_{i}\hskip 8.50012pt{\Gamma}\vdash{b}{:}{T_{b}}\hskip 8.50012pt{\textsf{ty}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}})=T_{b}}{\{U_{a}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{\Sigma_{i\in I}(S_{i})}\triangleright{{\textbf{{connect}}}\;\ell_{j}(V)\;{\textbf{{to}}}\;{b}\;{\textbf{{as}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}{:}{\textbf{1}}\triangleleft{S^{\prime}_{j}}}

or

\footnotesize\frac{\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}{!!}{\ell_{j}}({A_{j}})\,{.}\,S^{\prime}_{j}\in\{S_{i}\}_{i}\hskip 8.24454pt{\Gamma}\vdash{b}{:}{T_{b}}\hskip 8.24454pt{\textsf{ty}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}})=T_{b}}{\{U_{a}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{\Sigma_{i\in I}(S_{i})}\triangleright{{\textbf{{connect}}}\;\ell_{j}(V)\;{\textbf{{to}}}\;{b}\;{\textbf{{as}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}{:}{\textbf{1}}\triangleleft{S^{\prime}_{j}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\{U_{a}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{\Sigma_{i\in I}(S_{i})}\triangleright{M}{:}{\textbf{1}}\triangleleft{S^{\prime}_{j}}}{\{U_{a}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{\Sigma_{i\in I}(S_{i})}\triangleright{{\textbf{{try}}}\>{\textbf{{connect}}}\;\ell_{j}(V)\;{\textbf{{to}}}\;{b}\;{\textbf{{as}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\>{\textbf{{catch}}}\>M}{:}{\textbf{1}}\triangleleft{S^{\prime}_{j}}}

.

Since the evaluation context will be discarded, WLOG we proceed assuming that

.

As

and therefore that

Again by Lemma

\{U_{b}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{\Sigma_{i\in I}({{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}{??}{\ell_{k}}({C_{k}})\,{.}\,{T_{k}})}\triangleright{{\textbf{{accept}}}\;{\textbf{{from}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\;\{\ell_{i}(x_{k})\mapsto M_{k}\}_{k\in K}}{:}{B^{\prime}}\triangleleft{T^{\prime}}

We now need to introduce the new runtime typing environment for the new session.
We begin with a singleton runtime typing environment
\{s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\emptyset\rangle{:}U_{a}\}
, and recall that

U_{a}=\{\Sigma_{i\in I}(S_{i})\}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}{!!}{\ell_{j}}({A_{j}})\,{.}\,S^{\prime}_{j}\in\{S_{i}\}_{i}
.

Since
\mathcal{C}
is defined wrt. a well-formed program and thus protocols
are well-formed,
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}
is a unique initiator, and we know that

and
{\textsf{safe}}(\{s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\emptyset\rangle{:}U_{a}\})

As a consequence of safety, we know that

.

We can then show a reduction on typing environments:

\footnotesize\frac{\frac{\exists j\in I.S_{j}={{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}{!!}{\ell_{j}}({A_{j}})\,{.}\,S^{\prime}_{j}\hskip 8.24454pt{\textsf{ty}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}})={{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}{??}{\ell_{k}}({C_{k}})\,{.}\,{T_{k}}\hskip 8.24454ptj\in K\hskip 8.24454ptC_{j}=A_{j}}{s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\emptyset\rangle{:}\Sigma_{i\in I}(S_{i})\xRightarrow{s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{::}\ell_{j}}s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\rangle{:}S^{\prime}_{j},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\rangle{:}T_{j}}}{s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\emptyset\rangle{:}\Sigma_{i\in I}(S_{i})\xRightarrow{s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{::}\ell_{j}}s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\rangle{:}S^{\prime}_{j},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\rangle{:}T_{j}}

{\textsf{safe}}(s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\rangle{:}S^{\prime}_{j},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\rangle{:}T_{j})
.

Noting that only top-level frames (i.e.,

) can contain exception-handling frames,

are pure.

Similarly, we can show

.

We can then show a reduction on typing environments:

\footnotesize\frac{\frac{\exists j\in I.S_{j}={{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}{!!}{\ell_{j}}({A_{j}})\,{.}\,S^{\prime}_{j}\hskip 8.24454pt{\textsf{ty}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}})={{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}{??}{\ell_{k}}({C_{k}})\,{.}\,{T_{k}}\hskip 8.24454ptj\in K\hskip 8.24454ptC_{j}=A_{j}}{s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle{:}\Sigma_{i\in I}(S_{i})\xRightarrow{s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{::}\ell_{j}}s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\rangle{:}S^{\prime}_{j},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\rangle{:}T_{j}}}{s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle{:}\Sigma_{i\in I}(S_{i})\xRightarrow{s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\twoheadrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{::}\ell_{j}}s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\rangle{:}S^{\prime}_{j},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\rangle{:}T_{j}}

(noting that as a consequence of safety, we know that
C_{j}=A_{j}
).

Since

s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle{:}\Sigma_{i\in I}(S_{i})\longrightarrow s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\rangle{:}S^{\prime}_{j},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\rangle{:}T_{j}
, it follows by safety that

{\textsf{safe}}(s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\rangle{:}S^{\prime}_{j},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\rangle{:}T_{j})
.

Noting that only top-level frames (i.e.,
F,F^{\prime}
) can contain exception-handling frames,

E,E^{\prime}
are pure.
We can show

\{U_{a}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S^{\prime}_{j}}\triangleright{{\textbf{{return}}}\>()}{:}{A}\triangleleft{S^{\prime}_{j}}
and so
by Lemma
27
,

\{U_{a}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S^{\prime}_{j}}\triangleright{E[{\textbf{{return}}}\>()]}{:}{\textbf{1}}\triangleleft{{\textsf{end}}}
.

Similarly, we can show

\{U_{b}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{T_{j}}\triangleright{M_{j}\{V/x_{j}\}}{:}{B^{\prime}}\triangleleft{T^{\prime}}

and so by Lemma

,

\{U_{b}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{T_{j}}\triangleright{E^{\prime}[M_{j}\{V/x_{j}\}]}{:}{B}\triangleleft{{\textsf{end}}}
.

Recomposing:

\footnotesize\frac{\frac{a:{\textsf{Pid}}(U_{a})\in\Gamma\hskip 8.24454pt\hskip 8.24454pt\{U_{a}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S^{\prime}_{j}}\triangleright{E[{\textbf{{connect}}}\;\ell_{j}(V)\;{\textbf{{to}}}\;{b}\;{\textbf{{as}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]}{:}{A}\triangleleft{{\textsf{end}}}\hskip 8.24454pt\{U_{a}\}\>\>{\Gamma}\vdash{\kappa_{1}}}{\Gamma;a:U_{a},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\rangle{:}S^{\prime}_{j}\vdash\langle a,E[{\textbf{{return}}}\>()],s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\rangle,\kappa_{1}\rangle}\hskip 8.50012pt\frac{b:{\textsf{Pid}}(U_{b})\in\Gamma\hskip 8.24454pt\{U_{b}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{T_{j}}\triangleright{E^{\prime}[M_{j}\{V/x_{j}\}]}{:}{B}\triangleleft{{\textsf{end}}}\hskip 8.24454pt\hskip 8.24454pt\{U_{b}\}\>\>{\Gamma}\vdash{\kappa_{2}}}{\Gamma;b:U_{b},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\rangle{:}T_{j}\vdash\langle b,E^{\prime}[M_{j}\{V/x_{j}\}],s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\rangle,\kappa_{2}\rangle}}{\Gamma;a:U_{a},b:U_{b},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\rangle{:}S^{\prime}_{j},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\rangle{:}T_{j}\vdash\langle a,E[{\textbf{{return}}}\>()],s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\rangle,\kappa_{1}\rangle\parallel\langle b,E^{\prime}[M_{j}\{V/x_{j}\}],s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\rangle,\kappa_{2}\rangle}

as required.

Case
E-Comm

Let
\Delta_{1}=a:U_{a},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle{:}S_{a}
and

.

Assumption:

\footnotesize\frac{\frac{a:{\textsf{Pid}}(U_{a})\in\Gamma\hskip 8.24454pt\hskip 8.24454pt\{U_{a}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S_{a}}\triangleright{E[{\textbf{{send}}}\;\ell_{j}(V)\;{\textbf{{to}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}]}{:}{A}\triangleleft{{\textsf{end}}}\hskip 8.24454pt\hskip 8.24454pt\{U_{a}\}\>\>{\Gamma}\vdash{\kappa_{1}}}{\Gamma;\Delta_{1}\vdash\langle a,E[{\textbf{{send}}}\;\ell_{j}(V)\;{\textbf{{to}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}],s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle,\kappa_{1}\rangle}\hskip 8.50012pt\frac{b:{\textsf{Pid}}(U_{b})\in\Gamma\hskip 8.24454pt\hskip 8.24454pt\{U_{b}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S_{b}}\triangleright{E^{\prime}[{\textbf{{receive}}}\;{\textbf{{from}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\;\{\ell_{i}(x_{i})\mapsto M_{i}\}_{i\in I}]}{:}{B}\triangleleft{{\textsf{end}}}\hskip 8.24454pt\hskip 8.24454pt\{U_{b}\}\>\>{\Gamma}\vdash{\kappa_{2}}}{\Gamma;\Delta_{2}\vdash\langle b,E^{\prime}[{\textbf{{receive}}}\;{\textbf{{from}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\;\{\ell_{i}(x_{i})\mapsto M_{i}\}_{i\in I}],s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{s}}}}\rangle,\kappa_{2}\rangle}}{\Gamma;\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\vdash\langle a,E[{\textbf{{send}}}\;\ell_{j}(V)\;{\textbf{{to}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}],s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle,\kappa_{1}\rangle\parallel\langle b,E^{\prime}[{\textbf{{receive}}}\;{\textbf{{from}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\;\{\ell_{i}(x_{i})\mapsto M_{i}\}_{i\in I}],s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{s}}}}\rangle,\kappa_{2}\rangle}

and

Consider the subderivation

\{U_{a}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S_{a}}\triangleright{E[{\textbf{{send}}}\;\ell_{j}(V)\;{\textbf{{to}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}]}{:}{A}\triangleleft{{\textsf{end}}}

\footnotesize\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}{!}{\ell_{j}}({A_{j}})\in\{S_{i}\}_{i\in I}\hskip 8.50012pt{\Gamma}\vdash{V}{:}{A_{j}}}{\{U_{a}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{\Sigma_{i\in I}(S_{i})}\triangleright{{\textbf{{send}}}\;\ell_{j}(V)\;{\textbf{{to}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}}{:}{\textbf{1}}\triangleleft{S_{j}}}

Next, consider the subderivation

\{U_{b}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S_{b}}\triangleright{E^{\prime}[{\textbf{{receive}}}\;{\textbf{{from}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\;\{\ell_{i}(x_{i})\mapsto M_{i}\}_{i\in I}]}{:}{B}\triangleleft{{\textsf{end}}}

Again by Lemma

\footnotesize\frac{(\{U_{b}\}\>\>\Gamma,x_{k}:B_{k}\,\mid\,{T_{k}}\triangleright{M_{i}}{:}{B}\triangleleft{T^{\prime}})_{k\in K}}{\{U_{b}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{\Sigma_{k\in K}({{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}{?}{\ell_{k}}({B_{k}})\,{.}\,T_{k})}\triangleright{{\textbf{{receive}}}\;{\textbf{{from}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\;\{\ell_{i}(x_{i})\mapsto M_{i}\}_{i\in I}}{:}{A}\triangleleft{T^{\prime}}}

From the assumption we know that
j\in I
. As a result of the two

{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}{!}{\ell_{j}}({A_{j}})\,{.}\,S^{\prime}_{j}\in\{S_{i}\}_{i\in I}

Case
E-Disconn

\langle a,E[{\textbf{{wait}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}],s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\rangle,\kappa_{1}\rangle\parallel\langle b,E^{\prime}[{\textbf{{disconnect}}}\;{\textbf{{from}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}],s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\rangle,\kappa_{2}\rangle\longrightarrow\langle a,E[{\textbf{{return}}}\>()],s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle,\kappa_{1}\rangle\parallel\langle b,E^{\prime}[{\textbf{{return}}}\>()],\bot,\kappa_{2}\rangle

:

\footnotesize\frac{}{\{U_{a}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{{\#}{\uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\,{.}\,S^{\prime}}\triangleright{{\textbf{{wait}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}{:}{\textbf{1}}\triangleleft{S^{\prime}}}

\footnotesize\frac{}{\{U_{b}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{{\#}{\downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}}\triangleright{{\textbf{{disconnect}}}\;{\textbf{{from}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}{:}{\textbf{1}}\triangleleft{T^{\prime}}}

T={\#}{\downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}

We can show a reduction on runtime typing environments:

\footnotesize\frac{\frac{\frac{}{s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\rangle{:}{\#}{\uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\,{.}\,S^{\prime}\xrightarrow{s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}{{\#}{\uparrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle{:}S^{\prime}}\hskip 8.24454pt\frac{}{s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\rangle{:}{\#}{\downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}\xrightarrow{s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}{{\#}{\downarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}\cdot}}{s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\rangle{:}{\#}{\uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\,{.}\,S^{\prime},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\rangle{:}{\#}{\downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}}\xRightarrow{s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}{\#}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}S^{\prime}}}{a:U_{a},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}\rangle{:}{\#}{\uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\,{.}\,S^{\prime},b:U_{b},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}\rangle{:}{\#}{\downarrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\xRightarrow{s{:}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}{\#}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}a:U_{a},s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle{:}S^{\prime},b:U_{b}}

Since

, and

{\textsf{safe}}(\Delta^{\prime})
.

Noting that only top-level frames (i.e.,

are pure.
We can show that
\{U_{a}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S^{\prime}}\triangleright{{\textbf{{return}}}\>()}{:}{\textbf{1}}\triangleleft{S^{\prime}}

\{U_{a}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{S^{\prime}}\triangleright{E[{\textbf{{return}}}\>()]}{:}{A}\triangleleft{S^{\prime}}

\footnotesize\frac{{\textsf{Pid}}(U_{a})\in\Gamma\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\{U_{a}\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{{\textsf{end}}}\triangleright{E^{\prime}[{\textbf{{return}}}\>()]}{:}{B}\triangleleft{{\textsf{end}}}\hskip 8.50012pt\{U_{a}\}\>\>{\Gamma}\vdash{\kappa_{2}}}{\Gamma;b:U_{a}\vdash\langle b,E^{\prime}[{\textbf{{return}}}\>()],\bot,\kappa_{2}\rangle}

, as required.

By Lemma

, there exists some

that:

\frac{}{\{T\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{{\#}{\uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\,{.}\,S^{\prime}}\triangleright{{\textbf{{wait}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}{:}{\textbf{1}}\triangleleft{S^{\prime\prime}}}

\frac{\frac{\{T\}\>\>\Gamma\,\mid\,{{\#}{\uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\,{.}\,S^{\prime}}\triangleright{E[{\textbf{{raise}}}]}{:}{A}\triangleleft{{\textsf{end}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\{T\}\>\>{\Gamma}\vdash{\kappa}}{\Gamma;a:T,s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle{:}{\#}{\uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\,{.}\,S^{\prime}\vdash\langle a,E[{\textbf{{wait}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}],s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle,\kappa\rangle}\quad\frac{}{\Gamma;s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{s}}}}\rangle{:}U\vdash\lightning s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]}}{\Gamma;a:T,s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle{:}{\#}{\uparrow}{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}}\,{.}\,S^{\prime}s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{s}}}}\rangle{:}U\vdash\langle a,E[{\textbf{{raise}}}],s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{p}}}]\langle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{r}}}}\rangle,\kappa\rangle\parallel{\rm ZAp}{s[{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\mathttbf{q}}}]}}

Case
